The moduli space of conically singular instantons over an SU(3)-manifold
Dominik Gutwein, Yuanqi Wang

Abstract
In this article we study the moduli space of conically singular instantons (or Hermitian Yang–Mills connections) with prescribed tangent connections over a 6-manifold equipped with an SU ​ ( 3 ) \textup{SU}(3) -structure. That is, we develop a Fredholm deformation theory for such SU ​ ( 3 ) \textup{SU}(3) -instantons in which we fix the tangent connection but allow the underlying principal bundle (and, in particular, the singular set) to vary. This leads to the existence of a Kuranishi structure for this moduli space. Moreover, we investigate the cokernel of the instanton deformation operator and give under certain assumptions a formula for its dimension. Ultimately, we apply our results to conically singular instantons with structure group ℙ ​ U ​ ( n ) \mathbb{P}\textup{U}(n) and give a formula for the virtual dimension of their moduli space in terms of sheaf cohomology of certain vector bundles over ℙ 2 \mathbb{P}^{2} .



1
Introduction

An
\textup{SU}(3)
-manifold is a (real) 6-manifold
Z
equipped with a symplectic and a holomorphic volume form
(\omega,\Omega)
, which are point-wise modelled upon the standard Kähler and holomorphic volume forms over
\mathbb{C}^{3}
(cf.
Section 2.1
). An instanton (called
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton in this article) over such an
\textup{SU}(3)
-manifold
(Z,\omega,\Omega)
is a connection
A
on a principal
G
-bundle whose curvature satisfies

\Lambda_{\omega}F_{A}=0\quad\textup{and}\quad F_{A}\wedge\textup{Im}\thinspace\Omega=0,

where
\Lambda_{\omega}
denotes the dual Lefschetz operator. Note that the holomorphic volume form
\Omega
induces an almost complex structure on
Z
and that the
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton equation is simply the Hermitian Yang–Mills condition
\Lambda_{\omega}F_{A}=0
and
F_{A}^{0,2}=0
(where we have tacitly assumed that the structure group
G
of the principal bundle is real).

In their highly influential article
[
17
]
Donaldson and Thomas initiated a program aimed to develop gauge theoretic invariants for special holonomy spaces in dimension 6, 7, and 8 that (formally) mirror the familiar Casson–Floer picture in dimension 2, 3, and 4 (see also
[
16
]
). These proposed invariants are based on the moduli space
\mathcal{M}\equiv\mathcal{M}_{\textup{SU}(3)/\textup{G}_{2}/\textup{Spin}(7)}
of instantons over the respective spaces (including
\textup{SU}(3)
-instantons in dimension 6). However, a rigorous definition of such invariants (even a complete understanding of the list of its ingredients) is met with great analytic difficulties due to various non-compactness phenomena related to the instanton equation (cf.
[
48
]
,
[
16
]
,
[
24
]
,
[
13
]
,
[
14
]
).

In dimension 6, when
(Z,\omega,\Omega)
is Calabi–Yau (i.e.
\omega
and
\Omega
are both closed and therefore parallel), the Donaldson–Uhlenbeck–Yau Theorem identifies the moduli space of
\textup{SU}(3)
-instantons with the moduli space of (slope (poly-) stable) locally free sheaves over
Z
and algebraic geometry may be used to compactify this space (cf.
[
46
, Chapter 3]
). In this situation, a holomorphic Casson invariant can be defined and is now known as the Donaldson–Thomas invariant in algebraic geometry (cf.
[
46
]
and
[
47
]
). However, when
(\omega,\Omega)
is non-integrable (or if one considers instantons over 7-dimensional
\textup{G}_{2}
- or 8-dimensional
\textup{Spin}(7)
-manifolds) these methods do not apply and one needs to construct a compactification
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
of the instanton moduli space geometric-analytically. The works of Uhlenbeck
[
50
]
, Price
[
41
]
, Nakajima
[
39
]
, and Tian
[
48
]
(see also
[
42
]
for a summary and
[
11
]
for an extension of Tian’s results) show that a sequence
([A_{n}])_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{M}
converges to another instanton
[A]
(in a
C^{\infty}_{\textup{loc}}
-sense outside the so-called blow-up locus) with the following two phenomena possibly occurring:

1.

Some of the energy of the instantons
([A_{n}])_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{M}
may get lost (in the limit) due to ASD-instantons bubbling of transverse to a (possibly singular) calibrated submanifold.

2.

The limiting instanton
[A]
may only be defined outside of a subset
S\subset Z
and may not be extendible over
S
. That is, the limiting instanton may have non-removable singularities.

The previous result shows that a compactification of
\mathcal{M}
needs to contain (amongst other data) the moduli space of singular instantons
\mathcal{M}_{\textup{sing}}\subset\partial\overline{\mathcal{M}}
as part of the boundary. However, little is currently known about such a space in general. In this paper, we construct and study the moduli space of such singular instantons, in the case where the singularities are isolated and of conical nature (and where the tangent connection has been fixed).

Conically singular instantons:
The simplest(non-trivial) singular set
S
for an instanton
A
consists of a finite number of isolated points
S=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{N}\}
. Around any point
s_{i}\in S
we may use a coordinate system
\Upsilon_{i}\colon\thinspace B_{R}(0)\to Z
centred at
s_{i}
to regard
\Upsilon_{i}^{*}A
as a connection over
B_{R}(0)\setminus\{0\}
. Rescaling this connection via a sequence
r_{n}\to 0
, one obtains a sequence of connections
\delta_{r_{n}}^{*}(\Upsilon_{i}^{*}A)
that converges (outside of the blow-up locus and up to taking subsequences and gauge transformations) to a radially invariant
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton
A_{s_{i}}
over
\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus S_{0}
(cf.
[
48
, Discussion prior to Lemma 5.3.1]
). The limit
A_{s_{i}}
is called a tangent connection or tangent cone for
A
at
s_{i}\in S
. Note, that
A_{s_{i}}
may not be unique even up to gauge transformations. We call
A
conically singular if for each
s_{i}\in S
, we may find such a tangent connection
A_{s_{i}}
that is defined over
\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
(i.e.
S_{0}=\{0\}
in the notation above) and if the convergence of
\Upsilon_{i}^{*}A
to
A_{s_{i}}
occurs at a polynomial rate, that is

\big|\nabla^{k}\big(\Upsilon_{i}^{*}A-A_{s_{i}}\big)\big|=\mathcal{O}(r^{\mu_{i}-k})\quad\textup{for every $k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$ as $r\to 0$}

for some
\mu_{i}>-1
. Note that due to the polynomial rate of convergence, the tangent connections
A_{s_{i}}
for a conically singular connection
A
are, in fact, unique up to gauge. (See also
[
55
]
,
[
26
]
, and
[
9
]
for conditions on
A
such that it is conically singular around
s_{i}\in S
. Moreover,
[
10
]
gives a complete algebraic geometric characterisation of the analytic tangent of a general singular Hermitian Yang–Mills connection over a Kähler manifold.)

The moduli space of conically singular instantons with prescribed tangent connections:
In this article we study the moduli space of conically singular instantons over an
\textup{SU}(3)
-manifold
(Z,\omega,\Omega)
. We hereby allow for varying underlying principal
G
-bundles and, in particular, varying singular sets of the instantons. However, for simplicity we fix a set of radially invariant connections over
\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
that we prescribe as the tangent connections of the singular instantons. That is, we pre-fix the model cone-connections that we wish to exhibit at each singular point. A ’full’ moduli theory should of course allow for varying tangent cones and we have added remarks on our expectations regarding the generalisations of our results to such a comprehensive moduli theory (cf.
Section 4
,
Section 6.2.3
, and
Section 7
).

The main contribution of the article at hand is the development of a Fredholm deformation theory for conically singular
\textup{SU}(3)
-instantons (with prescribed tangent cones) with which we prove the existence of a Kuranishi structure for the aforementioned moduli space (see also
Section 1
for a precise statement and the next paragraph for a detailed summary of our results). The difficulty when allowing varying underlying principal
G
-bundles is to show that the space of bundles up to suitable isomorphisms is finite dimensional. As an intermediate step we therefore introduce in
Section 3
the moduli space of
framed
conically singular connections in which an (ungeometric) choice of framing has been added to the collected data and carefully investigate the deformations of the underlying framed bundle modulo isomorphisms. This study together with the well-known Fredholm theory of conically singular elliptic differential operators then leads to the deformation theory of conically singular instantons (with prescribed tangent connections).

Note that the methods used to develop said deformation theory are not specific to 6-manifolds with
\textup{SU}(3)
-structures but should apply to other instantons as well. In fact,
Section 4
together with the results proven in
[
44
, Chapter 1]
should immediately give rise to a Kuranishi structure for the moduli spaces of conically singular
\textup{G}_{2}
- and
\textup{Spin}(7)
-instantons with prescribed tangent connections.

Summary and statement of results:
We begin in
Section 2
by reviewing the necessary background on
\textup{SU}(3)
-structures and dilation-invariant
\textup{SU}(3)
-instantons over
\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
which subsequently serve as singularity models for more general conically singular instantons. In
Section 2.3
we give the definition of (framed) conically singular connections over a fixed bundle and prove in
Section 2.3
that the set of compatible framings for such a conically singular connection is (essentially) a torsor over the compact Lie group given in (
2.8
). Note that the
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton equation is a priori overdetermined. We therefore prove in
Section 2.3
that whenever the
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure
(\omega,\Omega)
on
Z
satisfies
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}^{*}\omega=0
and
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\Omega=w_{1}\omega^{2}
for some
w_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
, then the
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton equation can be augmented to an elliptic system (modulo gauge equivalence).

We subsequently begin our study of the moduli space of conically singular
\textup{SU}(3)
-instantons. For this, we define in
Section 3
the moduli space of framed conically singular connections and describe its local structure. In
Section 4
these results are then extended to the space of (unframed) conically singular connections. In
Section 5
we finally define the moduli space of conically singular
\textup{SU}(3)
-instantons and use our local description of the moduli space of conically singular connections together with the well-known Fredholm theory of conically singular elliptic differential operators to show that this instanton moduli space admits a Kuranishi structure. More precisely, we prove:

Theorem A

(cf.
Section 5.2.2
and
Section 5.3
)
.

Assume that the
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure
(\omega,\Omega)
on
Z
satisfies
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}^{*}\omega=0
and
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\Omega=w_{1}\omega^{2}
for some
w_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
(so that the
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton equation can be augmented to an elliptic system) and let
G
be a compact Lie group with finite center. Moreover, fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
and for every
i=1,\dots,N
a principal
G
-bundle
\pi_{i}\colon\thinspace P_{i}\to S^{5}
together with an irreducible connection
1
1

1

In fact, it suffices that all
A_{i}
are infinitesimally irreducible, i.e. the only elements
\xi_{i}\in\Omega^{0}(S^{5},\mathfrak{g}_{P_{i}})
satisfying
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A_{i}}\xi_{i}=0
are
\xi_{i}=0
. However, then one needs to include the (discrete) stabiliser groups of the conically singular instanton into the statement (cf.
Section 5.2.2
).

A_{i}\in\mathcal{A}(P_{i})
satisfying (
2.6
). For
\mu\in(-1,\bar{\mu}_{1})\times\dots\times(-1,\bar{\mu}_{N})
, where
\bar{\mu}_{i}\coloneqq\min\{((-1,0)\cap\mathcal{D}(L_{A_{i}}))\cup\{0\}\}
(with
\mathcal{D}(L_{A_{i}})
as in
Section 5.2.1
), let
\mathcal{M}_{\mu}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
be the moduli space of conically singular
\textup{SU}(3)
-instantons with
N
singularities and prescribed tangent connections
\{(P_{i},A_{i})_{i=1,\dots,N}\}
as defined and topologised in
Section 5.1
.

1.

For every
[\mathbb{A}]\in\mathcal{M}_{\mu}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
there exist two finite dimensional vector spaces
W_{1},W_{2}
and a smooth map
\textup{ob}_{\mathbb{A}}\colon\thinspace W_{1}\to W_{2}
with
\textup{ob}_{\mathbb{A}}(0)=0
, such that a neighbourhood of
[\mathbb{A}]
in
\mathcal{M}_{\mu}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
is homeomorphic to a neighbourhood of
0
in
\textup{ob}_{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}(0)
.

2.

For
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
as in the previous point we have

\displaystyle\textup{virt-dim}\big(\mathcal{M}_{\mu}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})\big)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\dim W_{1}-\dim W_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}6+(8-\dim(\textup{Stab}_{\textup{SU}(3)}(A_{i})))-\hskip-20.0pt\sum_{\scriptscriptstyle\nu_{i}\in\mathcal{D}(L_{A_{i}})\cap(-5/2,\mu_{i})}\hskip-20.0pt\dim\mathcal{K}(L_{A_{i}})_{\nu_{i}}

where
\textup{Stab}_{\textup{SU}(3)}(A_{i})
is defined in (
2.8
) and
\mathcal{K}(L_{A_{i}})_{\nu_{i}}
in (
5.2
).

3.

The moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\mu}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
is homeomorphic to
\mathcal{M}_{\mu^{\prime}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
for any other rate
\mu^{\prime}\in(-1,\bar{\mu}_{1})\times\dots\times(-1,\bar{\mu}_{N})
.

Remark
.

The assumption that
G
has a finite center and that the tangent connections are (infinitesimally) irreducible makes the presentation in
Section 5.2.1
simpler. However,
[
44
, Chapter I.5]
shows that these conditions can be removed (see also
Section 5.2.1
and
Section 5.2.2
).

Remark
.

The assumption
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}^{*}\omega=0
and
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\Omega=w_{1}\omega^{2}
for some
w_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
on the
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure is used in the previous theorem to argue that the instanton equation may be augmented by introducing two further unknowns by
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Omega^{0}(Z,\mathfrak{g}_{P})
to the elliptic (modulo gauge) system

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\omega}F_{A}=0\quad\textup{and}\quad*(F_{A}\wedge\textup{Im}\thinspace\Omega)+\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A}\xi_{1}+J^{*}(\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A}\xi_{2})=0.

(1.1)

That is, if
(A,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
solves (
1.1
), then
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A}\xi_{i}=0
for
i=1,2
and
A
is an
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton, i.e.
A
solves the non-augmented equation (cf.
Section 2.3
). If the assumption on
(\omega,\Omega)
is dropped, then
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A}\xi_{i}=0
does not need to hold for solutions of (
1.1
) anymore, and the augmented equation becomes an honest equation for
(A,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
. If one is willing to accept (
1.1
) as an equation for
(A,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
, then the Fredholm deformation theory (in particular, the virtual-dimension formula) discussed in the previous theorem still applies. Note that Equation (
1.1
) could give rise to a symplectic approach to Donaldson–Thomas invariants (cf.
[
47
, Discussion at the beginning of Section 3]
,
[
45
]
, and
[
1
]
).

In
Section 6
we then study the obstruction space of a conically singular instanton, that is, the cokernel of its (full) deformation operator. More precisely, we define in
Section 6.1
a pairing between the cokernel of the instanton deformation operator over a fixed bundle and the deformation space of the underlying bundle. We then explain in
Section 6.2.3
how (under certain assumptions) some of the obstructions arising from the deformation problem over a fixed bundle may be overcome by deforming the underlying bundle. This is used in
Section 6.2.3
to show that under the same assumptions the (non-positive) virtual dimension of
\mathcal{M}_{\mu}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
given in
Section 1
is precisely the negative of the dimension of the obstruction space.

Finally, in
Section 7
we consider instantons with structure group
G=\mathbb{P}\textup{U}(n)
and use the results of the second named authors in
[
53
]
and
[
54
]
to prove the following:

Theorem B

(cf.
Section 7
and
Section 7
)
.

Assume we are in the set-up of the previous theorem where now
G=\mathbb{P}\textup{U}(n)
(with
n>1
). The discussion prior to
Section 7
associates to each
(\pi_{i}\colon\thinspace P_{i}\to S^{5},A_{i})
a holomorphic vector bundle
E_{i}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
over
\mathbb{P}^{2}=S^{5}/\textup{U}(1)
. The virtual dimension of the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\mu}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
is then given by

\displaystyle\textup{virt-dim}\big(\mathcal{M}_{\mu}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}6

\displaystyle+(8-\dim(\textup{Stab}_{\textup{SU}(3)}(A_{i})))-2\mathrm{h}^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{2},\End E_{i})

\displaystyle-2\mathrm{h}^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{2},(\End E_{i})(-1))

where
\mathrm{h}^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{2},F)\coloneqq\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathrm{H}^{0,1}(\mathbb{P}^{2},F))
for any holomorphic vector bundle
F\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Because of
[
53
, Proposition 4.1]
this implies

\textup{virt-dim}\big(\mathcal{M}_{\mu}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})\big)\leq 0

with equality if and only if
G=\mathbb{P}\textup{U}(2)
and all tangent connections
(\pi_{i}\colon\thinspace P_{i}\to S^{5},A_{i})
are isomorphic to the pull back of the Fubini–Study connection
(\mathbb{P}\textup{U}(T\mathbb{P}^{2},h_{\textup{FS}})\to\mathbb{P}^{2},A_{\textup{FS}})
.

Remark
.

If
(Z,\omega,\Omega)
is Calabi–Yau, then (singular) instantons with structure group
\textup{U}(n)
correspond to slope (poly-) stable reflexive sheaves over
Z
(cf.
[
2
, Theorem 3]
). Moreover,
[
51
, Corollary 10]
proves that the expected dimension of the moduli space of reflexive sheaves over
Z
with fixed Chern-class is zero. Thus, our virtual dimension for the moduli space of conically singular instantons
2
2

2

note that while the previous theorem is stated for instantons with structure group
\mathbb{P}\textup{U}(n)
, one can show that the same virtual dimension formula also hold for instantons with structure group
\textup{U}(n)
(see also the remark after
Section 1
)
whose tangent connections are all modelled on the Fubini–Study connection over
T\mathbb{P}^{2}
agrees with the one given in
[
51
]
. For singular instantons whose tangents are not all modelled on the Fubini–Study connection, our virtual dimension is strictly negative (even after taking the deformations of the tangent connection into account; cf.
Section 7
). Of course, we only restrict to deformations that preserve the singularity, whereas
[
51
]
considers deformations as general reflexive sheaves. It appears to us that some of the deformations in
[
51
]
could possibly ’smooth out’ the corresponding singular instanton to a (degenerating family) of non-singular instantons.

Remark
.

With regard to the previous remark (or the observation that the expected dimension of the moduli space of
smooth

\textup{SU}(3)
-instantons is always zero) it seems interesting to investigate if and how conically singular instantons whose tangents are modelled on the Fubini–Study connection on
\mathbb{P}\textup{U}(T\mathbb{P}^{2},h_{\textup{FS}})
can appear as the limit of smooth instantons (possibly with higher instanton number).

We end this article by collecting in
Appendix A
numerous well-known facts about conically singular elliptic differential operators used throughout this article.

Comparison to previous results:
Prior to our article, the deformation problem of conically singular instantons has been considered in
[
52
]
and
[
44
]
for instantons over
\textup{G}_{2}
- and
\textup{Spin}(7)
-manifolds. Note that in contrast to the article at hand both references fixed the underlying bundle and the tangent cones of the singular instantons. The results obtained in the present paper are therefore an extension of the work in
[
52
]
and
[
44
]
. (In fact – as already mentioned above –
Section 4
together with the results in
[
44
, Chapter 1]
should give rise to the analogue of
Section 1
for the moduli spaces of conically singular
\textup{G}_{2}
- and
\textup{Spin}(7)
-instantons.) Moreover, the results in
Section 1
build on the work
[
54
]
and
[
53
]
of the second named author on the model operator for dilation invariant instantons over
\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
arising as pullbacks from
\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

The moduli theory of conically singular calibrated submanifolds, on the other hand, goes back to the work of Joyce
[
27
]
and has by now been developed for all classes of calibrated submanifolds appearing naturally inside exceptional holonomy manifolds (cf.
[
33
]
,
[
20
]
, and
[
6
]
). In fact, many of our results and definitions are inspired by their respective analogues for conically singular submanifolds. Note, however, that when working with conically singular connections the equivalence relation posed by bundle isomorphisms (compatible with the singular structure) introduces an additional difficulty not present in the deformation theory of submanifolds.

2
SU(3)-structures and conically singular SU(3)-instantons

In this section we first review the necessary background on
\textup{SU}(3)
-structures and set our conventions. We then discuss dilation-invariant
\textup{SU}(3)
-instantons over
\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
which serve as singularity models. In the final subsection we treat singular connections whose singularities are of conical nature.

2.1
SU(3)-structures

Throughout this article we consider
\mathbb{C}^{3}
with coordinates
z_{\alpha}=x_{\alpha}+ix_{\alpha+1}
for
\alpha=1,3,5
together with its standard Kähler and holomorphic volume forms

\omega_{0}=\tfrac{i}{2}\big(\mathop{}\!\textup{d}z_{1}\wedge\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\overline{z}_{1}+\mathop{}\!\textup{d}z_{2}\wedge\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\overline{z}_{2}+\mathop{}\!\textup{d}z_{3}\wedge\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\overline{z}_{3}\big),\quad\Omega_{0}=\mathop{}\!\textup{d}z_{1}\wedge\mathop{}\!\textup{d}z_{2}\wedge\mathop{}\!\textup{d}z_{3}.

(2.1)

Identifying
\mathbb{C}^{3}\cong\mathbb{R}^{6}
these become

\displaystyle\omega_{0}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{12}+\varepsilon^{34}+\varepsilon^{56}

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}

\displaystyle=\big(\varepsilon^{135}-\varepsilon^{245}-\varepsilon^{236}-\varepsilon^{146}\big)+i\big(-\varepsilon^{246}+\varepsilon^{235}+\varepsilon^{145}+\varepsilon^{136}\big),

where
\varepsilon^{1},\dots,\varepsilon^{6}
is the standard (dual) basis of
(\mathbb{R}^{6})^{*}
and
\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\coloneqq\varepsilon^{\alpha}\wedge\varepsilon^{\beta}\wedge\varepsilon^{\gamma}
.

Definition 2.1
.

An
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure on a (real)
6
-manifold
Z
is a tuple of differential forms

(\omega,\Omega)\in\Omega^{2}(Z)\times\Omega^{3}(Z,\mathbb{C})

(2.2)

such that over every point
z\in Z
, there exists a (real vector space-) isomorphism
f\colon\thinspace\mathbb{R}^{6}\to T_{z}Z
such that the pullback satisfies
f^{*}(\omega_{z},\Omega_{z})=(\omega_{0},\Omega_{0})
.

Remark 2.2
.

Since the stabiliser
\textup{Stab}_{\textup{GL}(\mathbb{R}^{6})}(\omega_{0},\Omega_{0})
of the pair
(\omega_{0},\Omega_{0})
in
\textup{GL}(\mathbb{R}^{6})
is equal to
\textup{SU}(3)
, the previous definition is equivalent to an
\textup{SU}(3)
-reduction of the principal frame bundle
\mathcal{F}(TZ)
(cf.
[
43
, Chapter 1]
). Given a pair
(\omega,\Omega)
as in the previous definition, then the corresponding
\textup{SU}(3)
-subbundle of
\mathcal{F}(TZ)
is defined by

\{(f\colon\thinspace\mathbb{R}^{6}\to T_{z}Z)\in\mathcal{F}(TZ)\mid f^{*}(\omega_{z},\Omega_{z})=(\omega_{0},\Omega_{0})\}.

In fact, the common stabiliser of
\omega_{0}
and
\textup{Re}\thinspace\Omega_{0}
in
\textup{GL}(\mathbb{R}^{6})
is already
\textup{SU}(3)
(cf.
[
3
, Proposition 3.1]
or
[
8
, Remark 31]
). We could have therefore equivalently defined an
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure to be a pair of differential forms
(\omega,\Psi)\in\Omega^{2}(Z)\times\Omega^{3}(Z)
which are over each point linearly equivalent to
(\omega_{0},\textup{Re}\thinspace\Omega_{0})
. Moreover, Banos
[
3
, Section 2 and 3]
(see also
[
8
, Appendix A]
) proved that up to isomorphism there are only two pairs consisting of a symplectic form
\tilde{\omega}\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{6})^{*}
and a complex volume form
\tilde{\Omega}\in\Lambda^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{6})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C}
(i.e.
\tilde{\Omega}
is decomposable and
\tilde{\Omega}\wedge\overline{\tilde{\Omega}}\neq 0
) over
\mathbb{R}^{6}
which satisfy

\tilde{\omega}\wedge\tilde{\Omega}=0\qquad\textup{and}\qquad\tfrac{1}{6}\tilde{\omega}^{3}=\tfrac{i}{8}\tilde{\Omega}\wedge\overline{\tilde{\Omega}}.

(2.3)

One of these pairs results in an
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure and the other one in an
\textup{SU}(1,2)
-structure. Thus, an
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure on
Z
is given by a symplectic form
\omega
and a complex volume form
\Omega
satisfying (
2.3
) such that the resulting inner product is positive definite.

Remark 2.3
.

As a consequence of the previous remark, an
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure induces an almost complex structure
J
and a compatible Riemannian metric
g
on
Z
such that
\omega(\cdot,\cdot)=g(J\cdot,\cdot).
With respect to this
J
, the no-where vanishing 3-form
\Omega
is of type
(3,0)
.

The following proposition decomposes the differentials
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\omega
and
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\Omega
into irreducible
\textup{SU}(3)
-representations and can be found in
[
12
]
or
[
30
]
. Its proof follows immediately from the decomposition of differential forms into primitives (cf.
[
25
, Proposition 1.2.30]
) and the identity
\Omega\wedge\omega=0
.

Proposition 2.4

(cf.
[
12
, Section 1]
or
[
30
, Section 2.1]
)
.

Let
(\omega,\Omega)
be an
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure on
Z
. Define
w_{1}\in C^{\infty}(Z,\mathbb{C})
and
w_{4},w_{5}\in\Omega^{1}(Z)
as

\displaystyle w_{1}\coloneqq-\tfrac{i}{6}\big\langle\!\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\omega,\overline{\Omega}\big\rangle_{\mathbb{C}},\quad w_{4}\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{2}\Lambda_{\omega}\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\omega,\quad\textup{and}\quad w_{5}\coloneqq-\tfrac{1}{2}i_{\textup{Re}\thinspace\Omega}\mathop{}\!\textup{d}(\textup{Re}\thinspace\Omega)

where
\Lambda_{\omega}
denotes the dual Lefschetz operator. Then there exists a primitive form
w_{2}\in\Omega^{2}(Z,\mathbb{C})
(i.e.
\Lambda_{\omega}w_{2}=0
) and a primitive form
w_{3}\in\Omega^{3}(Z)
that additionally satisfies
\langle\Omega,w_{3}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}=0
such that

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\omega

\displaystyle=\tfrac{3i}{4}(w_{1}\overline{\Omega}-\overline{w}_{1}\Omega)+w_{4}\wedge\omega+w_{3},

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\Omega

\displaystyle=w_{1}\omega^{2}+w_{2}\wedge\omega+w_{5}\wedge\Omega.

Remark 2.5
.

[
12
, Theorem 1.1]
identifies the tensors
w_{1},\dots,w_{5}
in the previous proposition with the five classes of the intrinsic torsion of the
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure.

Remark 2.6
.

In the following we will restrict to
\textup{SU}(3)
-structures for which
w_{2}=w_{4}=w_{5}=0
. This has the advantage that the overdetermined
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton equation can be augmented to an elliptic system modulo gauge (cf.
Section 2.3
). Note that
w_{4}=0
is equivalent to
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}(\omega^{2})=0
and therefore also to
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}^{*}\omega=0
. Thus,
w_{2}=w_{4}=w_{5}=0
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\textup{d}^{*}\omega

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\Omega

\displaystyle=w_{1}\omega^{2}.

Proposition 2.7
.

Assume that
(\omega,\Omega)
is an
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure on
Z
with
w_{2}=w_{4}=w_{5}=0
. Then there exists a
\vartheta\in\textup{U}(1)
such that
(\omega,\vartheta\cdot\Omega)
is an
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure which satisfies
w_{2}=w_{4}=w_{5}=0
and additionally
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\textup{Im}\thinspace\Omega=0
.

Proof.

In the previous remark we have seen that
w_{2}=w_{4}=w_{5}=0
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\omega^{2}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\Omega

\displaystyle=w_{1}\omega^{2}.

Applying the de Rham differential to the second equation and using the first, we find that
(\mathop{}\!\textup{d}w_{1})\wedge\omega^{2}=0
and therefore
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}w_{1}=0
(cf.
[
25
, Proposition 1.2.30]
). The complex valued function
w_{1}
is constant and there exists a
\vartheta\in\textup{U}(1)
such that
\vartheta\cdot w_{1}
is real. The modified
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure
(\omega,\vartheta\cdot\Omega)
now satisfies

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\omega^{2}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\textup{d}(\vartheta\cdot\Omega)

\displaystyle=\vartheta\cdot w_{1}\omega^{2}\in\Omega^{4}(Z,\mathbb{R})

which finishes the proof.
∎

We end this section with a definition for a distinguished coordinate chart that will be useful throughout this article.

Definition 2.8
.

An
\textup{SU}(3)
-coordinate chart centered at any
z\in Z
is a diffeomorphism
\Upsilon\colon\thinspace B_{R}(0)\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}\to U\subset Z
for some
R>0
such that
\Upsilon(0)=z
and
\Upsilon^{*}(\omega_{z},\Omega_{z})=(\omega_{0},\Omega_{0})
at
z
.

2.2
Dilation-invariant SU(3)-instantons over
\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}

This section discusses dilation-invariant
\textup{SU}(3)
-instantons over
\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
. It turns out that for dilation-invariant connections, the
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton equation reduces to an equation over
S^{5}
(and in certain cases further to an equation over
\mathbb{P}^{2}\coloneqq S^{5}/\textup{U}(1)
). First, we therefore recall some facts about the induced Sasaki–Einstein structure on
S^{5}
.

2.2.1
The canonical Sasaki–Einstein structure on
S^{5}

In this subsection we collect some well-known facts about the canonical Sasaki–Einstein structure on the unit sphere
S^{5}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
needed for the second part of this section.

1.

Let
X_{\theta}\in\Gamma(TS^{5})
be the infinitesimal generator of the canonical
\textup{U}(1)
-action on
S^{5}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
. Furthermore, denote by
\theta\coloneqq g_{S^{5}}(X_{\theta},\cdot)
its dual 1-form, where
g_{S^{5}}
is the standard metric on the unit sphere
S^{5}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
. In fact,
\theta
is a contact 1-form, which defines the standard contact distribution
H\coloneqq\ker\theta
on
S^{5}
, with
X_{\theta}
being its associated Reeb vector field.

2.

The contact distribution
H
is invariant under the canonical complex structure
J
on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
and we therefore obtain an induced complex structure
J_{1}
on
H
. Its associated Hermitian form
\omega_{1}\coloneqq g_{S^{5}}(J_{1}\cdot,\cdot)\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{2}H^{*})
is equal to the restriction of the Kähler form
\omega_{0}\in\Omega^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{3})
to
S^{5}
, that is,
\omega_{1}={\omega_{0}}_{|S^{5}}
. In fact, over
\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
we have

\omega_{0}=r\mathop{}\!\textup{d}r\wedge\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}\theta+r^{2}\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}\omega_{1}

(2.4)

where
\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}\colon\thinspace\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}\to S^{5}
denotes the radial projection.

3.

Let
\Lambda^{2,0}H^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\to S^{5}
be the bundle of (complexified) 2-covectors of
H
, which are of type
(2,0)
with respect to
J_{1}
. The restriction of the canonical bundle
\Lambda^{3,0}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}^{3}
to
S^{5}
splits as

\Lambda^{3,0}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}^{3}_{|S^{5}}\cong\underline{\mathbb{C}}\otimes(\Lambda^{2,0}H^{*}_{\mathbb{C}})

where the trivial bundle is generated by
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}r+i\theta
. Therefore, there exist
\omega_{2},\omega_{3}\in\Gamma_{S^{5}}(\Lambda^{2}H^{*}))
such that

\displaystyle\langle\omega_{2}+i\omega_{3}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{2,0}H^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle\textup{and}\quad\Omega_{0}

\displaystyle=r^{2}(\mathop{}\!\textup{d}r+ir\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}\theta)\wedge(\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}\omega_{2}+i\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}\omega_{3}).

4.

The quadruple
(\theta,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3})
defines a special
\textup{SU}(2)
-structure on
S^{5}
that can be regarded as a Sasaki–Einstein structure (cf.
[
21
, Section 3.1]
and the references therein).

5.

The quotient
S^{5}/\textup{U}(1)
is given by the complex projective space
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and the contact 1-form
\theta
defines a connection on the
\textup{U}(1)
-bundle
\textup{pr}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\colon\thinspace S^{5}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
(a complex Hopf-bundle) with horizontal distribution
H
. The differential of the projection
\textup{pr}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
restricts to a complex linear bundle isomorphism

(\textup{D}\textup{pr}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})_{|H}\colon\thinspace H\to\textup{pr}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}^{*}T\mathbb{P}^{2}

(where
H
is equipped with
J_{1}
and
T\mathbb{P}^{2}
with its standard complex structure). This implies that the pullback of the canonical bundle
\textup{pr}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}^{*}(\Lambda^{2,0}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2})
is isomorphic to
\Lambda^{2,0}H^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Moreover, one can verify that up to a positive factor (which we fix to be one)
\textup{pr}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}^{*}\omega_{\textup{FS}}=\omega_{1}
, where
\omega_{\textup{FS}}
denotes the Fubini–Study form on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. (Alternatively, one may simply define
\omega_{\textup{FS}}
by this equation.)

2.2.2
Dilation-invariant instantons over
\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}

In this section we consider
\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
equipped with its canonical flat (Calabi–Yau)
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure (
2.1
). Let
\pi\colon\thinspace P\to\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
be a principal
G
-bundle where
G
is a compact Lie group whose Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
has been equipped with an
Ad
-invariant inner product. We denote by
\mathcal{A}(P)
the set of connections on
P
.

Definition 2.9
.

A connection
A\in\mathcal{A}(P)
is called
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton if it satisfies

\Lambda_{\omega_{0}}F_{A}=0\quad\textup{and}\quad F_{A}\wedge\textup{Im}\thinspace\Omega_{0}=0

(2.5)

where
\Lambda_{\omega_{0}}
is the dual Lefschetz operator associated to
\omega_{0}
.

Remark 2.10
.

In the following we tacitly assume that
G
is a real Lie group. The second condition in the previous definition is then equivalent to
F_{A}^{0,2}=0
, where we have complexified the adjoint bundle
\mathfrak{g}_{P}
in order to project to the
(0,2)
-component.

We now restrict to bundles and connections which are pulled back from the unit sphere
S^{5}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
. For this recall the canonical Sasaki–Einstein structure
(\theta,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3})
on
S^{5}
discussed in
Section 2.2.1
.

Proposition 2.11
.

Assume that
\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}\pi\colon\thinspace\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}P\to\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
is the pullback of a bundle
\pi\colon\thinspace P\to S^{5}
. The pullback
\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A
of a connection
A\in\mathcal{A}(P)
is an
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton over
\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
if and only if
A
satisfies

\displaystyle F_{A}\wedge\omega_{i}=0\textup{ for $i=1,2,3$.}

(2.6)

Note that if the curvature satisfies
F_{A}\wedge\omega_{i}=0
for some
i=1,2,3
, then
i_{X_{\theta}}F_{A}=0
, where
X_{\theta}\in\Gamma(TS^{5})
denotes the Reeb vector field associated to
\theta
.

Proof.

The curvature of
\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A
over
\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
satisfies
F_{\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A}=\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}F_{A}
. By
[
25
, Proposition 1.2.30]
and (
2.4
) we therefore obtain that
\Lambda_{\omega_{0}}F_{\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A}=0
is equivalent to

F_{A}\wedge\omega_{1}\wedge\theta=0.

Similarly,
F_{\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A}\wedge\textup{Im}\thinspace\Omega_{0}=0
is equivalent to

F_{A}\wedge\omega_{3}=0\quad\textup{and}\quad F_{A}\wedge\omega_{2}\wedge\theta=0.

Since
a\mapsto a\wedge\omega_{3}
is an injective map for
a\in\Omega^{1}(S^{5},\mathfrak{g}_{P})
(cf.
[
25
, Proposition 1.2.30]
), the equation
F_{A}\wedge\omega_{3}=0
implies
i_{X_{\theta}}F_{A}=0
. The other two equations then reduce to
F_{A}\wedge\omega_{i}=0
for
i=1,2
.
∎

Recall from
Point 5
of
Section 2.2.1
that the quotient map
\textup{pr}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\colon\thinspace S^{5}\to S^{5}/\textup{U}(1)=\mathbb{P}^{2}
satisfies
\textup{pr}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}^{*}(\Lambda^{2,0}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2})=\langle\omega_{2}+i\omega_{3}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\textup{pr}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}^{*}\omega_{\textup{FS}}=\omega_{1}
, where
\omega_{\textup{FS}}\in\Omega^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
denotes the Fubini–Study form. Since the complexification of the bundle of self-dual forms is given by

(\Lambda^{2}_{+}T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{2})_{\mathbb{C}}\cong\Lambda^{2,0}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{C}\cdot\omega_{\textup{FS}}\oplus\Lambda^{0,2}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2}

(cf.
[
15
, Lemma 2.1.57]
), we immediately have:

Corollary 2.12
.

Let
A
be a connection on a bundle (with real structure group) over
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. The pullback of
A
to
S^{5}
satisfies (
2.6
) (that is, the pullback of
A
to
\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
is a dilation-invariant
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton) if and only if
A
is an ASD instanton over
\mathbb{P}^{2}
with respect to the Fubini–Study metric and the orientation induced by the complex structure.

The following partial converse to this corollary is due to Baraglia and Hekmati:

Proposition 2.13

(
[
4
, Proposition 2.8]
)
.

Let
\pi\colon\thinspace P\to S^{5}
be a principal
G
-bundle and assume that
G
has a trivial center. Furthermore, let
A\in\mathcal{A}(P)
be an irreducible connection (i.e. the only gauge transformation preserving
A
is the identity) that satisfies (
2.6
). Then there exists a principal
G
-bundle
\pi^{\prime}\colon\thinspace P^{\prime}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
and an ASD instanton
A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}(P^{\prime})
with
\textup{pr}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}^{*}P^{\prime}=P
and
\textup{pr}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}^{*}A^{\prime}=A
.

We end this section with the following important class of examples:

Example 2.14
.

Let
\pi\colon\thinspace E\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a holomorphic vector bundle which is slope-stable (or, more generally, slope-polystable) with respect to the Fubini–Study form
\omega_{\textup{FS}}
. The Donaldson–Uhlenbeck–Yau Theorem
[
18
, Theorem 1]
(and
[
49
, Main Theorem]
for general compact Kähler manifolds) gives rise to an Hermitian metric
h
on
E
and a projective unitary connection
A\in\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{P}\textup{U}(E,h))
that satisfies

\Lambda_{\omega_{\textup{FS}}}F_{A}=0\quad\textup{and}\quad F_{A}^{0,2}=0.

Since

(\Lambda^{2}_{+}T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{2})_{\mathbb{C}}\cong\Lambda^{2,0}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{C}\cdot\omega_{\textup{FS}}\oplus\Lambda^{0,2}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2}

(cf.
[
15
, Lemma 2.1.57]
),
Section 2.2.2
implies that the pullback of
A
to
\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
gives rise to a dilation-invariant
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton.

2.3
Conically singular connections

Let
Z^{6}
be a compact 6-manifold with an
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure
(\omega,\Omega)
. Assume that
S\subset Z
is a finite subset and
\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S
is a principal
G
-bundle where
G
is a compact Lie group whose Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
has been equipped with an
Ad
-invariant inner product. We denote the space of connections on
P
by
\mathcal{A}(P)
. For the following discussion we fix for every
s\in S

•

a rate
\mu_{s}\in(-1,0)
,

•

a principal
G
-bundle
\pi_{s}\colon\thinspace P_{s}\to S^{5}
,

•

a connection
A_{s}\in\mathcal{A}(P_{s})
that satisfies (
2.6
).

Definition 2.15
.

For each
s\in S
let
\Upsilon_{s}\colon\thinspace B_{R}(0)\to Z
be an
\textup{SU}(3)
-coordinate system (as in
Section 2.1
) centered at
s
and
\tilde{\Upsilon}_{s}\colon\thinspace\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}P_{s}\to P
be a bundle isomorphism covering
\Upsilon_{s}
. We call a connection
A\in\mathcal{A}(P)
conically singular with respect to
\{(\Upsilon_{s},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{s})\}_{s\in S}
with rates
\mu\coloneqq\{\mu_{s}\}_{s\in S}
and tangent cones
\{(P_{s},A_{s})\}_{s\in S}

if

\big|\nabla^{k}_{\textup{pr}^{*}_{S^{5}}A_{s}}(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{s}^{*}A-\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s})\big|=\mathcal{O}(r^{\mu_{s}-k})\quad\textup{for every $k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$ as $r\to 0$.}

(2.7)

The set of all such conically singular connections will be denoted by
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(P,\{\Upsilon_{s},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{s},A_{s}\})
. Finally, we will call a bundle isomorphism
\tilde{\Upsilon}_{s}\colon\thinspace\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}P_{s}\to P
covering an
\textup{SU}(3)
-coordinate system
\Upsilon_{s}
that satisfies (
2.7
) a framing of
(\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S,A)
at
s
.

Definition 2.16
.

A connection
A\in\mathcal{A}(P)
is called conically singular with rates
\mu\coloneqq\{\mu_{s}\}_{s\in S}
and tangent cones
\{(P_{s},A_{s})\}_{s\in S}
if there exists a set framings
\{(\Upsilon_{s},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{s})\}_{s\in S}
as in the previous definition, such that
A\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(P,\{\Upsilon_{s},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{s},A_{s}\})
. The set of all conically singular connections on
P
with given rates
\mu
and tangent cones
\{(P_{s},A_{s})\}_{s\in S}
will be denoted by
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}(P,\{P_{s},A_{s}\})
. Moreover,
A
is called a conically singular
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton if
A
is conically singular and satisfies (
2.5
).

Remark 2.17
.

The condition
\mu_{s}>-1
ensures that
\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}
is the (up to gauge unique) tangent cone connection of
A\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu}(P,\{P_{s},A_{s}\})
at
s
. If
A\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu}(P,\{P_{s},A_{s}\})
is a conically singular
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton, the tangent cones
A_{s}
necessarily need to satisfy (
2.6
).

Example 2.18
.

Let
(Z,\omega,\Omega)
be a compact Calabi--Yau 3-fold
3
3

3

Note that the results in this example hold for general compact Kähler manifolds (possibly without
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure) of any dimension. The relevant equation in this case is the Hermitian Yang–Mills equation
F_{A}^{0,2}=0
and
i\Lambda_{\omega}F_{A}=\lambda\cdot\textup{id}
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
(when
\lambda=0
this is equivalent to (
2.5
) in the presence of an
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure).
(i.e.
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\omega=0
and
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\Omega=0
) and
\mathcal{E}
be a reflexive sheaf on
Z
. Assume that
\mathcal{E}
is the sum of slope-stable reflexive sheaves, which are locally free on the complement of a finite set
S\subset Z
. Furthermore, assume that around each
s\in S
there are holomorphic coordinates
\varphi\colon\thinspace B_{R}(0)\to Z
with
\varphi(0)=s
such that
\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}=\textup{pr}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}^{*}\mathcal{F}
, where
\textup{pr}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\colon\thinspace B_{R}(0)\setminus\{0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
is the canonical projection and
\mathcal{F}
is a locally free sheaf on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
which is the sum of slope-stable locally free sheaves. Bando and Siu
[
2
, Theorem 3]
proved that the holomorphic vector bundle
E\to Z\setminus S
associated to
\mathcal{E}_{|Z\setminus S}
admits an Hermitian inner product
h_{E}
and a projective unitary connection
A\in\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{P}\textup{U}(E,h_{E}))
, which is an
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton. Moreover, Jacob, Sá Earp, and Walpuski proved
[
26
, Theorem 1.2]
that for every
s\in S
there exists a rate
\mu_{s}>-1
and a connection
A_{s}\in\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{P}\textup{U}(F,h_{F}))
, where
F\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
is the vector bundle associated to
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathbb{P}\textup{U}(F,h_{F})
is the projective unitary bundle assoicated to a suitable Hermitian inner product
h_{F}
, such that
A
is conically singular with rate
\{\mu_{s}\}
and tangent cones given by the respective pullbacks of
\{(\mathbb{P}\textup{U}(F,h_{F}),A_{s})\}
to
S^{5}
.

Assume that
A\in\mathcal{A}(P)
is conically singular and that
s\in S
is a singular point. The following proposition shows that the set of framings
(\Upsilon_{s},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{s})
at
s
(as in
Section 2.3
), is up to terms of order
\mathcal{O}(r^{\mu_{s}+1})
an
\textup{Stab}_{\textup{SU}(3)}(A_{s})
-torsor, where

\textup{Stab}_{\textup{SU}(3)}(A_{s})\coloneqq\{\tilde{U}\colon\thinspace P_{s}\xrightarrow{\sim}P_{s}\mid\textup{$\tilde{U}$ covers an element in $\textup{SU}(3)$ and $\tilde{U}^{*}A_{s}=A_{s}$}\}.

(2.8)

Proposition 2.19
.

Let
A\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu}(P,\{P_{s},A_{s}\})
be a conically singular connection. Furthermore, let
\Upsilon_{i}\colon\thinspace B_{R}(0)\to Z
for
i=1,2
be two
\textup{SU}(3)
-coordinate systems both centered at (the same)
s\in S
and
\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}\colon\thinspace\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}P_{s}\to\Upsilon_{i}^{*}P
be two isomorphisms that both satisfy (
2.7
). Then there exists a bundle isomorphism
\tilde{U}\colon\thinspace P_{s}\to P_{s}
covering
U\coloneqq D_{0}(\Upsilon_{2}^{-1}\circ\Upsilon_{1})\in\textup{SU}(3)
such that
\tilde{U}^{*}A_{s}=A_{s}
and

\big|\nabla_{\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}}^{k}\big(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2}^{-1}\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1}-\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}\tilde{U}\big)\big|=\mathcal{O}(r^{\mu_{s}+1-k})\quad\textup{for every $k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$ as $r\to 0$.}

(2.9)

Here, we assumed that
G
is a subgroup of
\textup{GL}(W)
for some vector space
W
and
\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2}^{-1}\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1}
and
\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}\tilde{U}
can therefore both be regarded as vector bundle homomorphisms
P_{s}\times_{G}W\to P_{s}\times_{G}W
. Moreover, we concatenate
\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2}^{-1}\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1}
with the parallel transport over the straight line that connects any
(\Upsilon_{2}^{-1}\circ\Upsilon_{1})(z)
with
Uz
. Then both homomorphisms are sections of the linear bundle
\textup{Hom}(P_{s}\times_{G}W,U^{*}P_{s}\times_{G}W)
, so that the difference and the covariant derivatives are indeed well-defined.

Proof.

By pre-composing
\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2}
with the parallel transport over the straight line that connects any
Uz\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
with
(\Upsilon_{2}^{-1}\circ\Upsilon_{1})(z)
, we may in the following assume that
\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2}^{-1}\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1}
covers
U
. Note that since
\mu_{s}<1
, the modified
\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2}
still satisfies (
2.7
).

Since the isomorphisms
\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1}
and
\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2}
both satisfy (
2.7
), we have for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

\displaystyle\big|\nabla_{\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}}^{k}\big((\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2}^{-1}\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1})^{*}\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}-\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}\big)\big|

\displaystyle=\big|\nabla_{\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}}^{k}\big(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1}^{*}(A+a)-\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}\big)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\big|\nabla_{\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}}^{k}\big(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1}^{*}A-\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}\big)\big|+\big|\nabla_{\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}}^{k}\big(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1}^{*}a\big)\big|

where
a\coloneqq(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2}^{-1})^{*}\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}-A
satisfies
|\nabla^{k}(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2}^{*}a)|=\mathcal{O}(r^{\mu_{s}-k})
. Therefore,

\big|\nabla_{\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}}^{k}\big((\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2}^{-1}\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1})^{*}\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}-\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}\big)\big|=\mathcal{O}(r^{\mu_{s}-k})\quad\textup{for every $k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$ as $r\to 0$.}

(2.10)

Next, we define for sufficiently small
r>0
the following 1-parameter family of bundle isomorphisms on
P_{s}\to S^{5}
:

\tilde{g}_{r}\coloneqq\tilde{\delta}_{r}^{*}(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2}^{-1}\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1})_{|S^{5}_{r}}=\tilde{\delta}^{-1}_{r}\circ(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2}^{-1}\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1})_{|S^{5}_{r}}\circ\tilde{\delta}_{r}

where
\delta_{r}\colon\thinspace S_{1}^{5}\to S^{5}_{r}
for
r\in(0,R)
denotes the dilation map from the sphere of radius 1 onto the sphere of radius
r
and
\tilde{\delta}_{r}
is its canonical lift to
\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}P_{s}
. Equation (
2.10
) implies that for every
k\geq 1
:

\big|\nabla^{k}_{A_{s}}\tilde{g}_{r}\big|=\mathcal{O}(r^{\mu_{s}+1}).

Since the
C^{0}
-norm of
\tilde{g}_{r}
is bounded due to the compactness of
G
, the Arzelà–Ascoli Theorem implies that there exists an isomorphism
\tilde{U}\colon\thinspace P_{s}\to P_{s}
covering
U=D_{0}(\Upsilon_{2}^{-1}\circ\Upsilon_{1})
such that
\tilde{g}_{r}\to\tilde{U}
in
C^{\infty}
on
S^{5}
as
r\to 0
. Furthermore, since
\mu_{s}+1>0
, the isomorphism
\tilde{U}
is parallel with respect to
A_{s}
.

Equation (
2.10
) with
k=0
implies
|\partial_{r}\tilde{g}_{r}|=\mathcal{O}(r^{\mu_{s}})
and therefore

\big|\tilde{g}_{r}-\tilde{U}\big|\leq\int_{0}^{r}\big|\partial_{t}\tilde{g}_{t}\big|\mathop{}\!\textup{d}t=\mathcal{O}(r^{\mu_{s}+1}).

Dilating back, this implies (
2.9
) with
k=0
. Since
\tilde{U}
is parallel, Equation (
2.9
) with
k\geq 1
follows from (
2.10
) with
k-1
.
∎

If
(W_{\rho},\rho)
is any
G
-representation, then we will denote in the following by
P\times_{\rho}W_{\rho}
its associated vector bundle.

Definition 2.20
.

Let
\{(\Upsilon_{s},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{s})\}_{s\in S}
be a set of framings as in
Section 2.3
. For any set of rates
\lambda\coloneqq\{\lambda_{s}\}_{s\in S}\in\mathbb{R}^{S}
and any
G
-representation
(W_{\rho},\rho)
let

\displaystyle\Omega^{\ell}_{\lambda}(Z\setminus S,P\times_{\rho}W_{\rho};\{\Upsilon_{s},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{s}\})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big\{\eta\in\Omega^{\ell}(Z\setminus S,P\times_{\rho}W_{\rho})\ \big|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\big|\nabla^{k}_{\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}}(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{s}^{*}\eta)\big|=\mathcal{O}(r^{\lambda_{s}-k})\textup{ for $\forall s\in S$}\big\}

where the norm and the connection on
\Upsilon_{s}^{*}(\Lambda^{\ell}T^{*}Z)\cong\Lambda^{\ell}T^{*}(B_{R}(0)\setminus\{0\})
are induced by the flat metric on
B_{R}(0)\setminus\{0\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
.

Remark 2.21
.

Whenever the set
\{(\Upsilon_{s},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{s})\}_{s\in S}
of
\textup{SU}(3)
-coordinate systems and bundle isomorphisms in the previous definition is clear from the context, then we will remove it from the notation and simply write
\Omega^{\ell}_{\lambda}(Z\setminus S,P\times_{\rho}W_{\rho})
.

The following proposition shows that the definition of
\Omega^{\ell}_{\lambda}(Z\setminus S,P\times_{\rho}W_{\rho};\{\Upsilon_{s},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{s}\})
only depends on the
\times_{s\in S}\textup{Stab}_{\textup{SU}(3)}(A_{s})
-orbit of
\{(\Upsilon_{s},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{s})\}_{s\in S}
, where
\textup{Stab}_{\textup{SU}(3)}(A_{s})
was defined in (
2.8
).

Proposition 2.22
.

Let
\Upsilon_{i}\colon\thinspace B_{R}(0)\to Z
for
i=1,2
be two
\textup{SU}(3)
-coordinate systems both centered at (the same)
s\in S
and
\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}\colon\thinspace\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}P_{s}\to\Upsilon_{i}^{*}P
be two isomorphisms such that there exists a bundle isomorphism
\tilde{U}\colon\thinspace P_{s}\to P_{s}
covering
U\coloneqq D_{0}(\Upsilon_{2}^{-1}\circ\Upsilon_{1})\in\textup{SU}(3)
with
\tilde{U}^{*}A_{s}=A_{s}
and

\big|\nabla_{\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}}^{k}\big(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2}^{-1}\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1}-\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}\tilde{U}\big)\big|=\mathcal{O}(r^{\mu_{s}+1-k}).

(2.11)

Assume that
\eta\in\Omega^{\ell}(Z\setminus S,P\times_{\rho}W_{\rho})
satisfies
|\nabla^{k}_{\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}}(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1}^{*}\eta)|=\mathcal{O}(r^{\lambda_{s}-k})
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and some
\lambda_{s}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
\eta
satisfies also
|\nabla^{k}_{\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}}(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2}^{*}\eta)|=\mathcal{O}(r^{\lambda_{s}-k})
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Proof.

Dropping the subscript
\textup{pr}^{*}A_{s}
from the covariant derivative for notational convenience, we obtain

\displaystyle|\nabla^{k}(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2}^{*}\eta)|

\displaystyle\leq|\nabla^{k}((\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1}^{-1}\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2}-\tilde{U})^{*}\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1}^{*}\eta)|+|\nabla^{k}(\tilde{U}^{*}\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1}^{*}\eta)|

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(r^{\mu_{s}+1+\lambda_{s}-k})+\mathcal{O}(r^{\lambda_{s}-k}).

Since
\mu_{s}+1>0
, this term is
\mathcal{O}(r^{\lambda_{s}-k})
.
∎

We end this section by showing that whenever the
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure
(\omega,\Omega)
on
Z
satisfies
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}^{*}\omega=0
and
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\Omega=w_{1}\omega^{2}
for
w_{1}=-\frac{i}{6}\langle\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\omega,\overline{\Omega}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\in\mathbb{C}
, then the
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton equation can be augmented to an elliptic equation modulo gauge.

Proposition 2.23
.

Fix a conically singular connection
A\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu}(P,\{P_{s},A_{s}\})
and define
\Omega^{\ell}_{\mu}(Z\setminus S,\mathfrak{g}_{P})
with respect to any framing
\{(\Upsilon_{s},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{s})\}_{s\in S}
of
A
. Assume that
(\omega,\Omega)
satisfies
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}^{*}\omega=0
and
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\Omega=w_{1}\omega^{2}
. Then
A+a
for
a\in\Omega^{1}_{\mu}(Z\setminus S,\mathfrak{g}_{P})
is a conically singular
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton in Coulomb gauge relative to
A
if and only if there are
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Omega^{0}_{\mu}(Z\setminus S,\mathfrak{g}_{P})
such that

\Lambda_{\omega}F_{A+a}=0,\quad*(F_{A+a}\wedge\textup{Im}\thinspace\Omega)+\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A+a}\xi_{1}+J^{*}(\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A+a}\xi_{2})=0,\quad\textup{and}\quad\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A}^{*}a=0

(2.12)

where
J
is the almost complex structure associated to
(\omega,\Omega)
(cf.
Section 2.1
). Moreover, the two sections
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
in the latter case satisfy
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A+a}\xi_{1}=\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A+a}\xi_{2}=0
.

Remark 2.24
.

Equation (
2.12
) is up to a zeroth-order term, which we have chosen to discard, the dimensional reduction of the
\textup{G}_{2}
-monopole equation in Coulomb gauge.

Proof.

First note that the previous proposition and
Section 2.3
imply that the definition of
\Omega^{\ell}_{\mu}(Z\setminus S,\mathfrak{g}_{P})
is independent of the chosen
\{(\Upsilon,\tilde{\Upsilon})\}_{s\in S}
.

If
A+a
is an
\textup{SU}(3)
-instanton in Coulomb gauge relative to
A
, then it satisfies the three equations with
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}=0
. Conversely, assume that
a,\xi_{1},
and
\xi_{2}
satisfy the equations in (
2.12
). Applying
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A+a}^{*}
to the second equation and using
*(F_{A+a}\wedge\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\textup{Im}\thinspace\Omega)=2\textup{Im}\thinspace(w_{1})\Lambda_{\omega}F_{A+a}=0
by the first equation,
\frac{1}{2}*(\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A+a}\xi_{2}\wedge\omega\wedge\omega)=-J^{*}\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A+a}\xi_{2}
, and
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}(\omega\wedge\omega)=*\mathop{}\!\textup{d}^{*}\omega=0
we obtain

0=\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A+a}^{*}\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A+a}\xi_{1}-[\Lambda_{\omega}F_{A+a},\xi_{2}]=\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A+a}^{*}\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A+a}\xi_{1}.

An integration by parts argument, which is justified since
\langle\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A+a}\xi_{1},\xi_{1}\rangle=\mathcal{O}(r^{2\mu_{s}-1})
at any
s\in S
with
\mu_{s}>-1
, then gives
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A+a}\xi_{1}=0
. After further multiplication by
J^{*}
, the second equation reduces to

*(F_{A+a}\wedge\textup{Re}\thinspace\Omega)-\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A+a}\xi_{2}=0.

Again applying
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A+a}^{*}
and using integration by parts as above gives
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A+a}\xi_{2}=0
.
∎

Now define the map

\displaystyle\Theta_{A}\colon\thinspace

\displaystyle\Omega^{0}_{\mu}(Z\setminus S,\mathfrak{g}_{P}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{P}\oplus T^{*}Z\otimes\mathfrak{g}_{P})\to\Omega^{0}_{\mu}(Z\setminus S,\mathfrak{g}_{P}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{P}\oplus T^{*}Z\otimes\mathfrak{g}_{P})

\displaystyle(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},a)\mapsto\big(\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A}^{*}a,\Lambda_{\omega}F_{A+a},*(F_{A+a}\wedge\textup{Im}\thinspace\Omega)+\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A+a}\xi_{1}+J^{*}(\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A+a}\xi_{2})\big).

Then

\Theta_{A}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},a)=\Theta_{A}(0)+L_{A}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},a)+Q_{A}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},a)

for

\displaystyle L_{A}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A}^{*}\\
0&0&\Lambda_{\omega}\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A}\\
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A}&J^{*}\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A}&*(\textup{Im}\thinspace\Omega\wedge\mathop{}\!\textup{d}_{A})\end{pmatrix}

and

Q_{A}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},a)\coloneqq\big(0,\tfrac{1}{2}\Lambda_{\omega}[a\wedge a],*(\tfrac{1}{2}[a\wedge a]\wedge\textup{Im}\thinspace\Omega)+[a,\xi_{1}]+J^{*}[a,\xi_{2}]\big).

The following proposition follows from a straight forward calculation similar to the proof of
Section 2.3
.

Proposition 2.25
.

Let
(\omega,\Omega)
be an
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure with
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}^{*}\omega=0
and
\mathop{}\!\textup{d}\Omega=w_{1}\omega^{2}
. The linear operator
L_{A}
is elliptic and its formal adjoint is given by

L_{A}^{*}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},a)=L_{A}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},a)+(0,0,2\textup{Im}\thinspace(w_{1})J^{*}a).

Thus, if
w_{1}
is real, then
L_{A}
is formally self-adjoint.

Remark 2.26
.

In the following, we will always assume that
\textup{Im}\thinspace(w_{1})=0
so that
L_{A}
is formally self-adjoint. By
Section 2.1
this does not pose a (significant) additional restriction on the
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure.

3
The moduli space of framed conically singular connections

The aim of the upcoming sections is to define the moduli space of conically singular
\textup{SU}(3)
-instantons with prescribed tangent cones and to describe its local structure. As a first step, this section investigates the local structure of the space of conically singular connections in which the framing
\{(\Upsilon_{s},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{s})\}
in
Section 2.3
is taken as part of the collected data.

3.1
Definition of the space and its topology

Throughout this section,
Z^{6}
is a compact 6-manifold with an
\textup{SU}(3)
-structure
(\omega,\Omega)
. Furthermore,
G
is a compact Lie group whose Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
has been equipped with an
Ad
-invariant inner product.

First, we define an equivalence relation on the set of framings
\{(\Upsilon_{s},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{s})\}_{s\in S}
appearing in
Section 2.3
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus\{s\}
be a principal
G
-bundle and let
\pi_{s}\colon\thinspace P_{s}\to S^{5}
and
A_{s}\in\mathcal{A}(P_{s})
be chosen. Furthermore, let
\Upsilon_{1}\colon\thinspace B_{R_{1}}(0)\to Z
and
\Upsilon_{2}\colon\thinspace B_{R_{2}}(0)\to Z
be two
\textup{SU}(3)
-coordinate systems centered at
s
and
\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}\colon\thinspace\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}P_{s}\to P
be two framings covering
\Upsilon_{i}
, respectively. We call
(\Upsilon_{1},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1})
and
(\Upsilon_{2},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2})
equivalent at rate
\mu_{s}\in\mathbb{R}
with respect to
(P_{s},A_{s})
if
D_{0}\Upsilon_{1}=D_{0}\Upsilon_{2}
and

|\nabla^{k}_{\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{s}}(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{2}^{-1}\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{1}-\textup{Id})|=\mathcal{O}(r^{\mu_{s}+1-k})\quad\textup{for every $k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$ as $r\to 0$.}

Definition 3.2

(
Moduli space of framed connections
)
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be the number of singular points,
\mu\coloneqq\{\mu_{i}\}_{i\in\{1,\dots,N\}}
for
\mu_{i}\in(-1,0)
be a set of rates, and
\{(P_{i},A_{i})\}_{i\in\{1,\dots,N\}}
be a set of (prescribed) tangent cones. With these we define the following:

1.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
be the set consisting of elements of the form:

\big(S,\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S,\{[\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}]\}_{i\in\{1,\dots,N\}},A\big)

where

•

S=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{N}\}\subset Z
is a totally ordered subset,

•

\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S
is a principal
G
-bundle,

•

for each
i=1,\dots,N
,
[\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}]
is an equivalence class (with respect to the relation in
Section 3.1
) of

–

an
\textup{SU}(3)
-coordinate system
\Upsilon_{i}\colon\thinspace B_{R}(0)\to Z
centered at
s_{i}
,

–

a bundle isomorphism
\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}\colon\thinspace\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}P_{i}\to\Upsilon_{i}^{*}P

at rate
\mu_{i}
with respect to
(P_{i},A_{i})
.

•

A\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(P,\{\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i},A_{i}\})
(as in
Section 2.3
, where
(\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i})
is any representative of the equivalence class
[\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}]
).

2.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})\coloneqq\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})/\sim
where the equivalence relation
\sim
is defined by

\big(S,\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S,\{[\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}]\}_{i=1,\dots,N},A\big)\sim\big(S^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}\colon\thinspace P^{\prime}\to Z\setminus S^{\prime},\{[\Upsilon_{i}^{\prime},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}^{\prime}]\}_{i=1,\dots,N},A^{\prime}\big)

if
S=S^{\prime}
(as ordered sets),
D_{0}(\Upsilon_{i}^{-1}\circ\Upsilon_{i}^{\prime})=\textup{Id}
for all
i=1,\dots,N
, and there exists an isomorphism
F\colon\thinspace P^{\prime}\to P
(covering the identity) that satisfies

•

\big|\nabla_{\textup{pr}^{*}_{S^{5}}A_{s_{i}}}^{k}\big(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}^{-1}\circ F\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}^{\prime}-\textup{Id}\big)\big|=\mathcal{O}(r^{\mu_{i}+1-k})
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
i=1,\dots,N
,

•

F^{*}A=A^{\prime}
.

In the formulation above we again assumed that
G
is a subgroup of
\textup{GL}(W)
for some vector space
W
and
\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}^{-1}\circ F\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}^{\prime}
can therefore be regarded as a vector bundle homomorphism
P_{i}\times_{G}W\to P_{i}\times_{G}W
. Moreover, we use parallel transport over straight lines in order to regard
\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}^{-1}\circ F\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}^{\prime}
as a section of the linear bundle
\End(P_{s}\times_{G}W)
, so that the difference and the covariant derivatives are well-defined.

Remark 3.3
.

First, note that the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(P,\{\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i},A_{i}\})
in
Section 2.3
remains unchanged when replacing any pair
(\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i})
with an equivalent (in the sense of
Section 3.1
) pair
(\Upsilon_{i}^{\prime},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}^{\prime})
. For the definition of
\mathcal{A}^{\textup{Fr}}_{\mu}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
the choice of representative of
[\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}]
is therefore irrelevant.

Remark 3.4
.

In the following we will equip the spaces in the previous definition with a topology such that they are non-connected. If one wishes to restrict to a connected component, then one should additionally assume that all bundles in the definition above are isomorphic to a fixed bundle via an isomorphism that covers a diffeomorphism that is isotopic to the identity.

Remark 3.5
.

In the previous definition we take the singular set
S
to be totally ordered because we want to prescribe the tangent cone
(P_{i},A_{i})
at each singularity in advance. A ’full’ moduli theory of conically singular connections (and instantons) should of course take the tangent cone around any singularity as part of the data collected in
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}
. As a step toward such a moduli space with variable tangent cones, one should also identify a singular connection

(S,\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S,\{[\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}]\}_{i\in\{1,\dots,N\}},A)

in the definition of
\mathcal{B}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
given above with

(\sigma(S),\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S,\{[\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}]\}_{i\in\{1,\dots,N\}},A),

where
\sigma\in S_{N}
is any permutation that satisfies
\mu_{i}=\mu_{\sigma(i)}
and
(P_{i},A_{i})=(P_{\sigma(i)},A_{\sigma(i)})
for every
i=1,\dots,N
and
\sigma(S)\coloneqq\{\sigma(s_{1}),\dots,\sigma(s_{N})\}
(as a totally ordered set). Note, however, that because the symmetric group
S_{N}
is finite, dividing out this additional group action will not change the local structure of
\mathcal{B}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
.

Next, we define topologies on
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
and
\mathcal{B}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
. For this we first define a topology on the set
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(P,\{\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i},A_{i}\})
of all conically singular connections on a fixed bundle with respect to a fixed set of framings
\{(\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i})\}
.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
S\coloneqq\{s_{1},\dots,s_{N}\}\subset Z
be a totally ordered set, and let
\{(P_{i},A_{i})\}_{i=1,\dots,N}
and
\{(\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i})\}_{i=1,\dots,N}
be as in
Section 2.3
. Furthermore, we assume that each coordinate system
\Upsilon_{i}
is defined over a ball
B_{R}(0)\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
of the same radius
R
. For any
\lambda\coloneqq\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N}\}
we define
\rho\colon\thinspace Z\setminus S\to(0,\infty)
(a distance function) and
w_{\lambda}\colon\thinspace Z\to\mathbb{R}
(a possibly non-continuous rate function) via

\displaystyle\rho(x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\big|\Upsilon_{i}^{-1}(x)\big|\quad&\textup{if $x\in\Upsilon_{i}(B_{R}(0))$,}\\
R&\textup{else,}\end{cases}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad w_{\lambda}(x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\lambda_{i}\quad&\textup{if $x\in\Upsilon_{i}(B_{R}(0))$,}\\
-1&\textup{else.}\end{cases}

For some fixed
A_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(P,\{\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i},A_{i}\})
, we define the
C^{\infty}_{\mu}
-topology on
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(P,\{\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i},A_{i}\})
to be the topology generated by the following set of semi-norms:

|A_{0}+a|_{C^{{k}}_{{\mu}}{}}\coloneqq\sup|\rho^{-w_{\mu-k}}\nabla_{A_{0}}^{k}a|\quad\textup{where $k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$ and $A_{0}+a\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(P,\{\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i},A_{i}\})$}

(where
\mu-k\coloneqq\{\mu_{1}-k,\dots,\mu_{N}-k\}
).

Remark 3.7
.

A moment’s thought reveals that the definition of the
C^{\infty}_{\mu}
-topology is independent of the choice of radius
R>0
and base connection
A_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(P,\{\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i},A_{i}\})
. Moreover, if
\{(\Upsilon_{i}^{\prime},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i=1,\dots,N}
is another set of framings which is equivalent to
\{(\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i})\}_{i=1,\dots,N}
in the sense of
Section 3.1
, then
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(P,\{\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i},A_{i}\})=\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(P,\{\Upsilon_{i}^{\prime},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}^{\prime},A_{i}\})
as topological spaces (equipped with their respective
C^{\infty}_{\mu}
-topologies).

With the
C^{\infty}_{\mu}
-topology at hand, we now define topologies on
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
and
\mathcal{B}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
.

Definition 3.8
.

For fixed rates
\mu=\{\mu_{i}\}_{i=1,\dots,N}
and tangent cones
\{(P_{i},A_{i})\}_{i=1,\dots,N}
, let
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
and
\mathcal{B}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
be as in
Section 3.1
. We first define the following collection
\mathcal{C}
of subsets of
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
which will subsequently serve as the basis for a topology.

Let
\mathbb{A}\coloneqq(S,\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S,\{[\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}]\},A)\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
be any element. Furthermore, assume that we have have chosen

•

An open neighbourhood
V_{1}\subset\{f\in\textup{Diff}(Z)\mid f^{*}(\omega,\Omega)_{f(s_{i})}=(\omega,\Omega)_{s_{i}}
for all
i=1,\dots,N
and
S=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{N}\}
} of the identity (with respect to the
C^{\infty}
-topology). Furthermore, we assume
\textup{dist}(s_{i},f(s_{i}))<\varepsilon
for every
f\in V_{1}
and
s_{i}\in S
where
\varepsilon\ll\textup{dist}(s_{i},s_{j})
for all
s_{i}\neq s_{j}\in S
.

•

An open neighbourhood
V_{2}\subset\times_{i=1}^{N}\textup{Stab}_{\textup{SU}(3)}(A_{i})
of the identity (with respect to the
C^{\infty}
-topology) where
\textup{Stab}_{\textup{SU}(3)}{(A_{i})}
is as in (
2.8
).

•

An open neighbourhood
V_{3}\subset\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(P,\{\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i},A_{i}\})
of
A
with respect to the
C^{\infty}_{\mu}
-topology.

We then define
V_{\mathbb{A}}(V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})\subset\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
as

\displaystyle V_{\mathbb{A}}(V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})\coloneqq\Big\{\big(S^{\prime},f\circ\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S^{\prime},\{[f\circ\Upsilon_{i}\circ U,\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}

\displaystyle\circ\tilde{U}_{i}]\}_{i},A^{\prime}\big)\in\mathcal{A}^{\textup{Fr}}_{\mu}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})\ \Big|

\displaystyle(f,\{U_{i},\tilde{U}_{i}\}_{i},A^{\prime})\in V_{1}\times V_{2}\times V_{3}\Big\}

and
\mathcal{C}
as the collection of all such subsets, i.e.
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\cup V_{\mathbb{A}}(V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})
(where the union is taken over all
\mathbb{A}
,
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
, and
V_{3}
as above).

We now equip
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}
with the topology generated by
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}
with the quotient topology.

Remark 3.9
.

Let
S\subset Z
be a subset and
\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S
be a principal
G
-bundle. Furthermore, let
f\colon\thinspace Z\to Z
be a diffeomorphism with
S^{\prime}\coloneqq f(S)
. The principal
G
-bundle
f\circ\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S^{\prime}
(where the total space
P
and the
G
-action are the same as for
\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S
and the projection is concatenated with
f
) is isomorphic to the push-forward bundle
f_{*}\pi\colon\thinspace f_{*}P\to Z\setminus S^{\prime}
. This isomorphism identifies the (same) connection
A
(now considered over
f\circ\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S^{\prime}
) with the push-forward connection over
f_{*}P
. Thus, the neighbourhood
V_{\mathbb{A}}(V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})\in\mathcal{C}
defined above consists of conically singular connections on the push-forward bundles of
P
by certain diffeomorphisms. Moreover, note that by
Section 3.1
, the definition of
V_{\mathbb{A}}(V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})
is independent of the choice of representative for the equivalence classes
\{[\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}]\}_{i=1,\dots,N}
.

Proposition 3.10
.

The collection
\mathcal{C}
in the previous definition is closed under finite intersections and defines therefore a basis for the topology on
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{A}\coloneqq(S,\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S,\{[\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}]\},A)
be an element in
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
and let
V_{\mathbb{A}}(V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})\subset\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
be an open neighbourhood (associated to open sets
V_{1},V_{2},V_{3}
) of the form described in the previous definition. We will first show that for any
\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\in V_{\mathbb{A}}(V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})
there exist
V_{1}^{\prime}
,
V_{2}^{\prime}
,
V_{3}^{\prime}
as in the previous definition such that
V_{\mathbb{A}}(V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})=V_{\mathbb{A}^{\prime}}(V_{1}^{\prime},V_{2}^{\prime},V_{3}^{\prime})
.

For this, note that by the definition of
V_{\mathbb{A}}(V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})
any
\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\in V_{\mathbb{A}}(V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})
can be written as

\mathbb{A}^{\prime}=\big(f(S),f\circ\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S,\{[f\circ\Upsilon_{i}\circ U_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}\circ\tilde{U}_{i}]\},A^{\prime}\big)

where
(f,\{(U_{i},\tilde{U}_{i})\},A^{\prime})\in V_{1}\times V_{2}\times V_{3}
. Since any element in
V_{1}
can be written as
(f^{\prime}\circ f^{-1})\circ f
and any element in
V_{2}
can be written as
\{(U_{i},\tilde{U}_{i})\}\circ\{(U_{i}^{-1}\circ U_{i}^{\prime},\tilde{U}_{i}^{-1}\circ\tilde{U}_{i}^{\prime})\}
, we immediately have
V_{\mathbb{A}}(V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})=V_{\mathbb{A}^{\prime}}(V_{1}^{\prime},V_{2}^{\prime},V_{3}^{\prime})
for
V_{1}^{\prime}\coloneqq V_{1}\circ f^{-1}
,
V_{2}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{(U_{i}^{-1},\tilde{U}_{i}^{-1})\}\circ V_{2}
, and
V_{3}^{\prime}\coloneqq V_{3}
.

In order to prove that
\mathcal{C}
is closed under intersections take two elements in
\mathcal{C}
which are not disjoint from one another. By the previous argument we may write these two sets as
V_{\mathbb{A}}(V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})
and
V_{\mathbb{A}}(V_{1}^{\prime},V_{2}^{\prime},V_{3}^{\prime})
for some
\mathbb{A}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
and
V_{1},V_{1}^{\prime},V_{2},V_{2}^{\prime},V_{3},V_{3}^{\prime}
as in
Section 3.1
. Then
V_{\mathbb{A}}(V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})\cap V_{\mathbb{A}}(V_{1}^{\prime},V_{2}^{\prime},V_{3}^{\prime})=V_{\mathbb{A}}(V_{1}\cap V_{1}^{\prime},V_{2}\cap V_{2}^{\prime},V_{3}\cap V_{3}^{\prime})
, and the result follows.
∎

3.2
The local structure of
\mathcal{B}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})

In this section we prove that
\mathcal{B}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
(as defined in the previous section) is locally homeomorphic to an open neighbourhood in a product of the form

(\mathbb{C}^{3})^{N}\times(\times_{i}\mathfrak{m}_{i})\times\big(\times_{i}\textup{Stab}_{\textup{SU}(3)}(A_{i})\times\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(P,\{\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i},A_{i}\})\big)/\mathcal{G}_{\mu+1}.

Here, the framed bundle
\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S
is fixed (but depends on the neighbourhood in
\mathcal{B}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
), the groups
\textup{Stab}_{\textup{SU}(3)}(A_{i})
are as in (
2.8
),
\mathfrak{m}_{i}\subset\mathfrak{su}(3)
is a subspace complementary to the image of (the Lie algebra)
\mathfrak{stab}_{\textup{SU}(3)}(A_{i})
under the canonical projection
\mathfrak{stab}_{\textup{SU}(3)}(A_{i})\to\mathfrak{su}(3)
, and
\mathcal{G}_{\mu+1}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mu+1}\coloneqq\big\{g\in\mathcal{G}(P)

\displaystyle\mid|\nabla^{k}(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}^{-1}\circ g\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}-\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}\tilde{U}_{i})|=\mathcal{O}(r^{\mu_{i}+1-k})\textup{ for every $i=1,\dots,N$,}

\displaystyle\quad\textup{$k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$, and a $\tilde{U}_{i}\in\mathcal{G}(P_{i})$ that preserves $A_{i}$}\big\}

(where we again assume that
G\subset\textup{GL}(W)
for some finite dimensional vector space
W
).

Geometrically, the elements in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
in the product above correspond to (locally) moving the singular points of
\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S
. Similarly, the elements in
\mathfrak{m}_{i}\subset\mathfrak{su}(3)
’rotate’ the bundle around
s_{i}
and elements in
\textup{Stab}_{\textup{SU}(3)}(A_{i})
correspond to changing the framing at
s_{i}
.

We begin in
Section 3.2.1
by showing that bundles of the form
f_{1}\circ\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus f_{1}(S)
and
f_{2}\circ\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus f_{2}(S)
, where
f_{1},f_{2}\colon\thinspace Z\to Z
are diffeomorphisms satisfying certain properties, may be identified in a way that is compatible with their conically singular structure. In
Section 3.2.2
we then define a suitable parametrisation of
\mathcal{B}_{\mu}^{\textup{Fr}}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
and prove that this parametrisation indeed defines a local homeomorphism.

3.2.1
Construction of suitable bundle isomorphisms

Recall from
Section 3.1
that the (framed) bundles
\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S
in a sufficiently small neighbourhood in
\mathcal{A}^{\textup{Fr}}_{\mu}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
are the push-forwards of a fixed bundle by certain diffeomorphisms close to the identity. When going to the quotient
\mathcal{B}^{\textup{Fr}}_{\mu}(\{P_{i},A_{i}\})
, we first have to determine which of these framed bundles are identified by an isomorphism that respects their respective conically singular structure.

It is well-known that two diffeomorphisms
f_{0},f_{1}\colon\thinspace Z\to Z
which are both sufficiently close to the identity and agree at the singular points
S
give rise to isomorphic push-forward bundles
f_{i}\circ\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus f_{i}(S)
. The following proposition shows that when
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
agree at
S
to first order, then this bundle isomorphism may be chosen to respect their singular structure.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
S=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{N}\}
be a totally ordered set and
\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus S
be a bundle together with a set of framings
\{(\Upsilon_{i},\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i})\}_{i=1,\dots,N}
around each
s_{i}\in S
as in
Section 2.3
. Assume that
f_{0},f_{1}\colon\thinspace Z\to Z
are two diffeomorphisms which are both sufficiently close with respect to the
C^{1}
-norm to the identity and satisfy

(f_{0})_{|S}=(f_{1})_{|S}\quad\textup{and}\quad(Df_{0})_{|S}=(Df_{1})_{|S}.

Then there exists an isomorphism

F\colon\thinspace(f_{0}\circ\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus f_{0}(S))\to(f_{1}\circ\pi\colon\thinspace P\to Z\setminus f_{1}(S))

covering the identity with the property that

|\nabla^{k}(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}^{-1}\circ F\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}-\textup{Id})|=\mathcal{O}(r^{1-k})

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and every
i=1,\dots,N
.

Proof.

Since
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
are sufficiently close with respect to the
C^{1}
-norm and agree up to first order at
S
, there exists an isotopy of diffeomorphisms
f\colon\thinspace[0,1]\times Z\to Z
such that
(f_{t})_{|S}
and
(Df_{t})_{|S}
is constant in
t
(see for example
[
31
, Proof of Theorem 6.26]
).

We consider the principal
G
-bundle

\displaystyle\pi^{\prime}\colon\thinspace[0,1]\times P

\displaystyle\to[0,1]\times Z\setminus f_{0}(S)

\displaystyle(t,p)

\displaystyle\mapsto(t,f_{t}(\pi(p))).

(Note that this bundle is isomorphic to the pullback
h^{*}P
where
h\colon\thinspace[0,1]\times Z\setminus f_{0}(S)\to Z\setminus S
is the isotopy defined by
h_{t}\coloneqq f_{t}^{-1}
for every
t\in[0,1]
.) Equip
P
with a connection
A\in\mathcal{A}(P)
that satisfies
\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}^{*}A=\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}A_{i}
for every
i=1,\dots,N
. Its pullback
A^{\prime}\coloneqq\textup{pr}_{P}^{*}A
defines a connection on
\pi^{\prime}\colon\thinspace[0,1]\times P\to[0,1]\times Z\setminus f_{0}(S)
.

Now define
F\colon\thinspace P\to P
by

F(p)\coloneqq\textup{pr}_{P}(\textup{tra}_{[0,1]}^{A^{\prime}}(0,p))

where
\textup{tra}_{[0,1]}^{A^{\prime}}(0,p)
denotes the parallel transport of
(0,p)
on
\pi^{\prime}\colon\thinspace[0,1]\times P\to[0,1]\times Z\setminus f_{0}(S)
with respect to
A^{\prime}
over the path

[0,1]\ni t\mapsto(t,f_{0}(\pi(p)))\in[0,1]\times Z\setminus f_{0}(S).

In order to prove

|\nabla^{k}(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}^{-1}\circ F\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}-\textup{Id})|=\mathcal{O}(r^{1-k})

we first make the following definitions: Let
\pi_{i}^{\prime}\colon\thinspace[0,1]\times\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}P_{i}\to[0,1]\times B_{R}(0)\setminus\{0\}
be the pullback bundle which we equip with the pullback connection
A^{\prime}_{i}\coloneqq\textup{pr}_{P_{i}}^{*}A_{i}
. Furthermore, define for
z\in B_{R}(0)
and
t\in[0,1]

\displaystyle f_{0}^{\prime}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq(\Upsilon_{i}^{-1}\circ f_{0}\circ\Upsilon_{i})(z)

\displaystyle h_{t}^{\prime}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq(\Upsilon_{i}^{-1}\circ h_{t}\circ\Upsilon_{i})(z)

where
h_{t}=f_{t}^{-1}
. Then

(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}^{-1}\circ F\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i})(p)=\textup{pr}_{\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}P_{i}}(\textup{tra}^{A^{\prime}_{i}}_{[0,1]}(0,p))

where
\textup{tra}^{A^{\prime}_{i}}_{[0,1]}
is the parallel transport with respect to
A^{\prime}_{i}
over the path
t\mapsto(t,h_{t}^{\prime}(f_{0}^{\prime}(\pi(p))))
. Since
f_{t}
agrees for all
t\in[0,1]
with
f_{0}
up to first order at
S
, we have
h_{t}^{\prime}(f_{0}^{\prime}(z))=z+\mathcal{O}(t|z|^{2})
. This implies that the
A^{\prime}_{i}
-horizontal lift of
\partial_{t}(t,h^{\prime}_{t}(f^{\prime}_{0}(z)))\in T_{(t,h^{\prime}_{t}(f^{\prime}_{0}(z)))}([0,1]\times B_{R}(0))
to
([0,1]\times\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}P_{i})\cong([0,1]\times(0,R)\times P_{i})
satisfies

\displaystyle\textup{Lift}^{A^{\prime}_{i}}\big(\partial_{t},(\partial_{t}h^{\prime})_{t}(f^{\prime}_{0}(z))\big)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\partial_{t},\partial_{t}|h^{\prime}_{t}(f^{\prime}_{0}(z))|,\textup{Lift}^{A_{i}}\big(\partial_{t}\tfrac{h^{\prime}_{t}(f^{\prime}_{0}(z))}{|h^{\prime}_{t}(f^{\prime}_{0}(z))|}\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle=(\partial_{t},0,0)+\mathcal{O}(|z|).

Since
\textup{tra}^{A^{\prime}_{i}}_{[0,1]}(0,p)
is the flow of this horizontal lift, we obtain
|\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}^{-1}\circ F\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}-\textup{Id}|=\mathcal{O}(r)
. In order to estimate the derivatives, we note that

\displaystyle|\nabla(\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i}^{-1}\circ F\circ\tilde{\Upsilon}_{i})|

\displaystyle\leq c|(\textup{tra}_{[0,1]}^{A^{\prime}_{i}}(0,\cdot))^{*}A_{i}^{\prime}-A_{i}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq c\int_{0}^{1}|(\textup{pr}_{S^{5}}^{*}F_{A_{i}})(\partial_{t}h^{\prime}_{t}(f_{0}^{\prime}(\cdot)),\cdot)|\mathop{}\!\textup{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(r^{-2+2}).

The higher derivatives can be estimated analogously.
∎

Remark 3.12
.

Note that the isomorphism
F
constructed in the previous proof is the time-1 flow of a time-dependent vector field
(X^{H}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\in\Gamma(TP)
over
P
given as follows: First define the time-dependent vector field
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\in\Gamma(TZ)
for
